========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m… not completely sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I still do some of the things I used to like, but I don’t know if I’m enjoying them in the same way, if that makes sense. Dance has always been such a big part of how I understand myself, so even when I’m feeling heavy, I can still recognize that movement matters to me. But lately I can’t always tell whether I’m enjoying it or just going through the motions because it’s what I know how to do.

Some things still feel good in little flashes, like a piece of music catching me off guard or a moment in rehearsal where my body remembers something before my mind gets in the way. But it’s not consistent. I wouldn’t say everything is completely flat, but it’s… muted, I guess. Like the volume is turned down.

I don’t know that there’s anything that feels exactly the way it used to. That’s the part that’s hard for me to pin down, because I can still appreciate things, I think. Like I can hear a song and know it’s beautiful, or I can be in a studio and know that I used to feel lit up by that particular kind of movement or rhythm. But the actual pleasure of it is… I don’t know, thinner? More distant.

Sometimes there are moments where something breaks through a little, like if the music is just right or I’m not overthinking and my body kind of takes over. But it doesn’t last, and it doesn’t feel as full as it used to. So I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure there are activities I enjoy in the old way right now. There are just pieces of enjoyment, little flashes, but not the same steady feeling I remember.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Absolutely no pleasure… I’m not sure. I don’t think I can say that cleanly, like, “this one thing gives me zero feeling.” It’s more that a lot of things feel dulled or kind of far away from me.

There are definitely activities where I’ll start them and realize I’m waiting for the feeling to come, and it just doesn’t really arrive the way it used to. Like I can be doing something familiar and I know, intellectually, that I used to love it, but in the moment it feels more like completing a task than receiving anything from it. But I don’t know if that’s the same as absolutely no pleasure.

I guess if you mean things that feel completely empty every single time, I haven’t noticed one clear category like that. It varies. Some days something gives me a tiny spark, and another day the same thing feels like nothing. So… not absolutely no pleasure, maybe. More like inconsistent, muted, hard to access.

More like inconsistent, muted, hard to access.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / dancer_or_choreographer / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… yes and no. I don’t feel like I’ve completely lost interest, because if I had, I don’t think I’d still be trying to do any of it. Dance, music, being in creative spaces—those things still matter to me, and I still sort of reach toward them automatically because they’ve been part of me for so long.

But the interest doesn’t feel as alive as it used to. It’s more like I remember caring, and I’m trying to stay connected to that version of myself. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do this,” or “This used to make me feel like myself,” and then when I actually do it, the feeling is faint or delayed or just not really there in the way I expect.

So I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped caring altogether. It’s not like I look at everything I used to love and feel nothing across the board. But I do feel less pulled toward things. Less excited. More like I have to remind myself to engage instead of naturally wanting to. It feels like the interest is still there somewhere, but it’s harder to access.

Like I get up and I’m supposed to be myself, supposed to move through the day, and there’s just this emptiness sitting in my chest. It’s been weighing on me enough that I thought I should talk to someone instead of just trying to push through it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, hollow kind of sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. Not just a bad week or a rough patch that passed. It kind of settled in and stayed. I keep trying to mark when it started exactly, like whether there was a clear before-and-after moment, but it’s more like I look back and realize I’ve been carrying this heaviness for most of the past year. So, yes—around nine months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're still able to enjoy activities or things that you used to like before this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I still do some of the things I used to like, but I don’t know if I’m enjoying them in the same way, if that makes sense. Dance has always been such a big part of how I understand myself, so even when I’m feeling heavy, I can still recognize that movement matters to me. But lately I can’t always tell whether I’m enjoying it or just going through the motions because it’s what I know how to do.\n\nSome things still feel good in little flashes, like a piece of music catching me off guard or a moment in rehearsal where my body remembers something before my mind gets in the way. But it’s not consistent. I wouldn’t say everything is completely flat, but it’s… muted, I guess. Like the volume is turned down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, the way you used to before this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there’s anything that feels exactly the way it used to. That’s the part that’s hard for me to pin down, because I can still appreciate things, I think. Like I can hear a song and know it’s beautiful, or I can be in a studio and know that I used to feel lit up by that particular kind of movement or rhythm. But the actual pleasure of it is… I don’t know, thinner? More distant.\n\nSometimes there are moments where something breaks through a little, like if the music is just right or I’m not overthinking and my body kind of takes over. Like I can be doing something familiar and I know, intellectually, that I used to love it, but in the moment it feels more like completing a task than receiving anything from it. It’s not like I look at everything I used to love and feel nothing across the board. But I do feel less pulled toward things. Less excited. More like I have to remind myself to engage instead of naturally wanting to. It feels like the interest is still there somewhere, but it’s harder to access.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / dancer_or_choreographer / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Worthlessness_Guilt\nmanifestation: \"I keep replaying the cancelled performances and telling myself I let the dancers, the funders, and the whole community down, even though people have been kinder about it than I deserve.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I mean… I try not to say it too dramatically, because I know people keep telling me I’m being hard on myself, but yes, I do.

I keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, almost like if I replay every decision enough times I’ll find the exact moment where I ruined it. And then I start thinking about the dancers, and how much they put into it, and the funders who trusted me, and the people in the community who were waiting for it or had some connection to it. It just feels like I let all of them down.

And the confusing part is that people have actually been kinder about it than I feel like I deserve. They’ll say, “These things happen,” or “We understand,” or they’ll try to reassure me, and I can hear the words, but I don’t really believe them all the way. I keep thinking they’re just being gracious, or they don’t want to make me feel worse, or they don’t fully understand how much I failed them.

So yes, I blame myself a lot. Maybe more than is useful. It’s not just regret, it feels like this heavy sense that I should have held everything together better.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.8, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3, F33.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is that I’ve been waking up with this really heavy, hollow kind of sadness. It’s hard to describe without sounding dramatic, but it feels like the studio lights are on and everything is technically there, but there’s no music in me anymore. Like I get up and I’m supposed to be myself, supposed to move through the day, and there’s just this emptiness sitting in my chest. It’s been weighing on me enough that I thought I should talk to someone instead of just trying to push through it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, hollow kind of sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. Not just a bad week or a rough patch that passed. It kind of settled in and stayed. I keep trying to mark when it started exactly, like whether there was a clear before-and-after moment, but it’s more like I look back and realize I’ve been carrying this heaviness for most of the past year. So, yes—around nine months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're still able to enjoy activities or things that you used to like before this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I still do some of the things I used to like, but I don’t know if I’m enjoying them in the same way, if that makes sense. Dance has always been such a big part of how I understand myself, so even when I’m feeling heavy, I can still recognize that movement matters to me. But lately I can’t always tell whether I’m enjoying it or just going through the motions because it’s what I know how to do.\n\nSome things still feel good in little flashes, like a piece of music catching me off guard or a moment in rehearsal where my body remembers something before my mind gets in the way. But it’s not consistent. I wouldn’t say everything is completely flat, but it’s… muted, I guess. Like the volume is turned down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, the way you used to before this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there’s anything that feels exactly the way it used to. That’s the part that’s hard for me to pin down, because I can still appreciate things, I think. Like I can hear a song and know it’s beautiful, or I can be in a studio and know that I used to feel lit up by that particular kind of movement or rhythm. But the actual pleasure of it is… I don’t know, thinner? More distant.\n\nSometimes there are moments where something breaks through a little, like if the music is just right or I’m not overthinking and my body kind of takes over. But it doesn’t last, and it doesn’t feel as full as it used to. So I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure there are activities I enjoy in the old way right now. There are just pieces of enjoyment, little flashes, but not the same steady feeling I remember.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel absolutely no pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: Absolutely no pleasure… I’m not sure. I don’t think I can say that cleanly, like, “this one thing gives me zero feeling.” It’s more that a lot of things feel dulled or kind of far away from me.\n\nThere are definitely activities where I’ll start them and realize I’m waiting for the feeling to come, and it just doesn’t really arrive the way it used to. Like I can be doing something familiar and I know, intellectually, that I used to love it, but in the moment it feels more like completing a task than receiving anything from it. But I don’t know if that’s the same as absolutely no pleasure.\n\nI guess if you mean things that feel completely empty every single time, I haven’t noticed one clear category like that. It varies. Some days something gives me a tiny spark, and another day the same thing feels like nothing. So… not absolutely no pleasure, maybe. More like inconsistent, muted, hard to access.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. I don’t feel like I’ve completely lost interest, because if I had, I don’t think I’d still be trying to do any of it. Dance, music, being in creative spaces—those things still matter to me, and I still sort of reach toward them automatically because they’ve been part of me for so long.\n\nBut the interest doesn’t feel as alive as it used to. It’s more like I remember caring, and I’m trying to stay connected to that version of myself. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do this,” or “This used to make me feel like myself,” and then when I actually do it, the feeling is faint or delayed or just not really there in the way I expect.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped caring altogether. It’s not like I look at everything I used to love and feel nothing across the board. But I do feel less pulled toward things. Less excited. More like I have to remind myself to engage instead of naturally wanting to. It feels like the interest is still there somewhere, but it’s harder to access.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I try not to say it too dramatically, because I know people keep telling me I’m being hard on myself, but yes, I do.\n\nI keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, almost like if I replay every decision enough times I’ll find the exact moment where I ruined it. And then I start thinking about the dancers, and how much they put into it, and the funders who trusted me, and the people in the community who were waiting for it or had some connection to it. It just feels like I let all of them down.\n\nAnd the confusing part is that people have actually been kinder about it than I feel like I deserve. They’ll say, “These things happen,” or “We understand,” or they’ll try to reassure me, and I can hear the words, but I don’t really believe them all the way. I keep thinking they’re just being gracious, or they don’t want to make me feel worse, or they don’t fully understand how much I failed them.\n\nSo yes, I blame myself a lot. Maybe more than is useful. It’s not just regret, it feels like this heavy sense that I should have held everything together better.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel absolutely no pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: Absolutely no pleasure… I’m not sure. I don’t think I can say that cleanly, like, “this one thing gives me zero feeling.” It’s more that a lot of things feel dulled or kind of far away from me.\n\nThere are definitely activities where I’ll start them and realize I’m waiting for the feeling to come, and it just doesn’t really arrive the way it used to. Like I can be doing something familiar and I know, intellectually, that I used to love it, but in the moment it feels more like completing a task than receiving anything from it. But I don’t know if that’s the same as absolutely no pleasure.\n\nI guess if you mean things that feel completely empty every single time, I haven’t noticed one clear category like that. It varies. Some days something gives me a tiny spark, and another day the same thing feels like nothing. So… not absolutely no pleasure, maybe. More like inconsistent, muted, hard to access.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. I don’t feel like I’ve completely lost interest, because if I had, I don’t think I’d still be trying to do any of it. Dance, music, being in creative spaces—those things still matter to me, and I still sort of reach toward them automatically because they’ve been part of me for so long.\n\nBut the interest doesn’t feel as alive as it used to. It’s more like I remember caring, and I’m trying to stay connected to that version of myself. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do this,” or “This used to make me feel like myself,” and then when I actually do it, the feeling is faint or delayed or just not really there in the way I expect.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped caring altogether. It’s not like I look at everything I used to love and feel nothing across the board. But I do feel less pulled toward things. Less excited. More like I have to remind myself to engage instead of naturally wanting to. It feels like the interest is still there somewhere, but it’s harder to access.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I try not to say it too dramatically, because I know people keep telling me I’m being hard on myself, but yes, I do.\n\nI keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, almost like if I replay every decision enough times I’ll find the exact moment where I ruined it. And then I start thinking about the dancers, and how much they put into it, and the funders who trusted me, and the people in the community who were waiting for it or had some connection to it. It just feels like I let all of them down.\n\nAnd the confusing part is that people have actually been kinder about it than I feel like I deserve. They’ll say, “These things happen,” or “We understand,” or they’ll try to reassure me, and I can hear the words, but I don’t really believe them all the way. I keep thinking they’re just being gracious, or they don’t want to make me feel worse, or they don’t fully understand how much I failed them.\n\nSo yes, I blame myself a lot. Maybe more than is useful. It’s not just regret, it feels like this heavy sense that I should have held everything together better.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead during these past nine months?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / dancer_or_choreographer / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly.

I don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like I’ve been sitting there deciding how I would hurt myself. But there have been moments where I’ve felt so tired of being inside my own head that I’ve thought, not exactly “I want to die,” but more like… “I don’t want to keep feeling this.” Or “I wish I could disappear for a while.” It’s hard for me to know if that counts.

And I feel nervous saying even that, because I don’t want it to sound more dramatic than it is. I haven’t been actively trying to hurt myself. It’s more passive, I guess—like wanting everything to stop, or wanting to not have to wake up and deal with the same weight again. But then another part of me is still aware that I don’t actually want to leave people or do something irreversible.

So… yes, maybe in a vague way at times, but not with a plan. More like thoughts of escape than thoughts of actually harming myself.

Not just a bad week or a rough patch that passed. It kind of settled in and stayed. So, yes—around nine months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're still able to enjoy activities or things that you used to like before this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that cleanly. Like the volume is turned down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, the way you used to before this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there’s anything that feels exactly the way it used to. That’s the part that’s hard for me to pin down, because I can still appreciate things, I think. the wired stretch when I was barely sleeping and racing through plans. I’m still showing up for essentials, but I’m not functioning like I normally do as a choreographer or teacher.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This whole pattern started after my ankle injury and has been going on for about two months now.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 9 months\n  manifestation: The depressed part has been present for about nine months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / dancer_or_choreographer / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S024\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. I don’t feel like I’ve completely lost interest, because if I had, I don’t think I’d still be trying to do any of it. Dance, music, being in creative spaces—those things still matter to me, and I still sort of reach toward them automatically because they’ve been part of me for so long.\n\nBut the interest doesn’t feel as alive as it used to. It’s more like I remember caring, and I’m trying to stay connected to that version of myself. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do this,” or “This used to make me feel like myself,” and then when I actually do it, the feeling is faint or delayed or just not really there in the way I expect.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped caring altogether. It’s not like I look at everything I used to love and feel nothing across the board. But I do feel less pulled toward things. Less excited. More like I have to remind myself to engage instead of naturally wanting to. It feels like the interest is still there somewhere, but it’s harder to access.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I try not to say it too dramatically, because I know people keep telling me I’m being hard on myself, but yes, I do.\n\nI keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, almost like if I replay every decision enough times I’ll find the exact moment where I ruined it. And then I start thinking about the dancers, and how much they put into it, and the funders who trusted me, and the people in the community who were waiting for it or had some connection to it. It just feels like I let all of them down.\n\nAnd the confusing part is that people have actually been kinder about it than I feel like I deserve. They’ll say, “These things happen,” or “We understand,” or they’ll try to reassure me, and I can hear the words, but I don’t really believe them all the way. I keep thinking they’re just being gracious, or they don’t want to make me feel worse, or they don’t fully understand how much I failed them.\n\nSo yes, I blame myself a lot. Maybe more than is useful. It’s not just regret, it feels like this heavy sense that I should have held everything together better.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead during these past nine months?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly.\n\nI don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like I’ve been sitting there deciding how I would hurt myself. But there have been moments where I’ve felt so tired of being inside my own head that I’ve thought, not exactly “I want to die,” but more like… “I don’t want to keep feeling this.” Or “I wish I could disappear for a while.” It’s hard for me to know if that counts.\n\nAnd I feel nervous saying even that, because I don’t want it to sound more dramatic than it is. I haven’t been actively trying to hurt myself. It’s more passive, I guess—like wanting everything to stop, or wanting to not have to wake up and deal with the same weight again. But then another part of me is still aware that I don’t actually want to leave people or do something irreversible.\n\nSo… yes, maybe in a vague way at times, but not with a plan. More like thoughts of escape than thoughts of actually harming myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your energy level or the way you move throughout the day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 9\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. My energy feels… unreliable, I guess. Like I can wake up and think, okay, I’m going to do one or two very basic things today, and then one small thing takes so much more out of me than it should.

Even answering one email to a dancer can leave me feeling wiped out, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud because it’s just an email. But I’ll sit there staring at it, trying to choose the right words, feeling the weight of it, and by the time I’ve sent it I feel like I’ve already used up most of what I had for the day.

And showering has become this whole thing too, especially with the ankle brace. It’s not just “take a shower.” It’s managing the brace, standing carefully, trying not to slip, drying off, getting everything back on, and afterward I sometimes feel like I need to lie down. But the frustrating part is that lying down doesn’t really recharge me the way I expect it to. I’ll rest, but I don’t feel restored. It’s more like I’m still drained, just horizontal.

So yes, my energy is lower. I don’t know if my movement is slower exactly, but I feel like everything takes more effort than it should.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is that I’ve been waking up with this really heavy, hollow kind of sadness. It’s hard to describe without sounding dramatic, but it feels like the studio lights are on and everything is technically there, but there’s no music in me anymore. Like I get up and I’m supposed to be myself, supposed to move through the day, and there’s just this emptiness sitting in my chest. It’s been weighing on me enough that I thought I should talk to someone instead of just trying to push through it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, hollow kind of sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. Not just a bad week or a rough patch that passed. It kind of settled in and stayed. I keep trying to mark when it started exactly, like whether there was a clear before-and-after moment, but it’s more like I look back and realize I’ve been carrying this heaviness for most of the past year. So, yes—around nine months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're still able to enjoy activities or things that you used to like before this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I still do some of the things I used to like, but I don’t know if I’m enjoying them in the same way, if that makes sense. Dance has always been such a big part of how I understand myself, so even when I’m feeling heavy, I can still recognize that movement matters to me. But lately I can’t always tell whether I’m enjoying it or just going through the motions because it’s what I know how to do.\n\nSome things still feel good in little flashes, like a piece of music catching me off guard or a moment in rehearsal where my body remembers something before my mind gets in the way. But it’s not consistent. I wouldn’t say everything is completely flat, but it’s… muted, I guess. Like the volume is turned down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, the way you used to before this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there’s anything that feels exactly the way it used to. That’s the part that’s hard for me to pin down, because I can still appreciate things, I think. Like I can hear a song and know it’s beautiful, or I can be in a studio and know that I used to feel lit up by that particular kind of movement or rhythm. But the actual pleasure of it is… I don’t know, thinner? More distant.\n\nSometimes there are moments where something breaks through a little, like if the music is just right or I’m not overthinking and my body kind of takes over. But it doesn’t last, and it doesn’t feel as full as it used to. So I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure there are activities I enjoy in the old way right now. There are just pieces of enjoyment, little flashes, but not the same steady feeling I remember.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel absolutely no pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: Absolutely no pleasure… I’m not sure. I don’t think I can say that cleanly, like, “this one thing gives me zero feeling.” It’s more that a lot of things feel dulled or kind of far away from me.\n\nThere are definitely activities where I’ll start them and realize I’m waiting for the feeling to come, and it just doesn’t really arrive the way it used to. Like I can be doing something familiar and I know, intellectually, that I used to love it, but in the moment it feels more like completing a task than receiving anything from it. But I don’t know if that’s the same as absolutely no pleasure.\n\nI guess if you mean things that feel completely empty every single time, I haven’t noticed one clear category like that. It varies. Some days something gives me a tiny spark, and another day the same thing feels like nothing. So… not absolutely no pleasure, maybe. More like inconsistent, muted, hard to access.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. I don’t feel like I’ve completely lost interest, because if I had, I don’t think I’d still be trying to do any of it. Dance, music, being in creative spaces—those things still matter to me, and I still sort of reach toward them automatically because they’ve been part of me for so long.\n\nBut the interest doesn’t feel as alive as it used to. It’s more like I remember caring, and I’m trying to stay connected to that version of myself. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do this,” or “This used to make me feel like myself,” and then when I actually do it, the feeling is faint or delayed or just not really there in the way I expect.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped caring altogether. It’s not like I look at everything I used to love and feel nothing across the board. But I do feel less pulled toward things. Less excited. More like I have to remind myself to engage instead of naturally wanting to. It feels like the interest is still there somewhere, but it’s harder to access.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I try not to say it too dramatically, because I know people keep telling me I’m being hard on myself, but yes, I do.\n\nI keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, almost like if I replay every decision enough times I’ll find the exact moment where I ruined it. And then I start thinking about the dancers, and how much they put into it, and the funders who trusted me, and the people in the community who were waiting for it or had some connection to it. It just feels like I let all of them down.\n\nAnd the confusing part is that people have actually been kinder about it than I feel like I deserve. They’ll say, “These things happen,” or “We understand,” or they’ll try to reassure me, and I can hear the words, but I don’t really believe them all the way. I keep thinking they’re just being gracious, or they don’t want to make me feel worse, or they don’t fully understand how much I failed them.\n\nSo yes, I blame myself a lot. Yes… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself consistently, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my body feels different, and my rehearsal clothes are looser than they used to be. Like leggings and tops that normally sit snugly are sort of hanging on me more, especially around my waist and hips.

Part of it was that earlier on, when I was really buzzing with ideas and trying to keep all these creative threads going, I’d just forget to eat. Not on purpose exactly — I’d look up and realize I’d skipped lunch, or I’d had coffee and maybe a few bites of something and somehow convinced myself that counted.

And now it’s almost the opposite, but the result is similar. I’ll know I should eat, but food just doesn’t have much appeal. It tastes kind of like cardboard, even things I usually like. I’ll make something and then just push it around or eat enough to say I ate. So yes, I think I’ve lost some weight over these months, even if I don’t know the exact amount.

I don’t feel like I’ve completely lost interest, because if I had, I don’t think I’d still be trying to do any of it. But I’ll sit there staring at it, trying to choose the right words, feeling the weight of it, and by the time I’ve sent it I feel like I’ve already used up most of what I had for the day.\n\nAnd showering has become this whole thing too, especially with the ankle brace. But the frustrating part is that lying down doesn’t really recharge me the way I expect it to. I’ll rest, but I don’t feel restored. It’s more like I’m still drained, just horizontal.\n\nSo yes, my energy is lower. I don’t know if my movement is slower exactly, but I feel like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself consistently, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my body feels different, and my rehearsal clothes are looser than they used to be. Like leggings and tops that normally sit snugly are sort of hanging on me more, especially around my waist and hips.\n\nPart of it was that earlier on, when I was really buzzing with ideas and trying to keep all these creative threads going, I’d just forget to eat. Not on purpose exactly — I’d look up and realize I’d skipped lunch, or I’d had coffee and maybe a few bites of something and somehow convinced myself that counted.\n\nAnd now it’s almost the opposite, but the result is similar. I’ll know I should eat, but food just doesn’t have much appeal. It tastes kind of like cardboard, even things I usually like. I’ll make something and then just push it around or eat enough to say I ate. So yes, I think I’ve lost some weight over these months, even if I don’t know the exact amount.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But lately I can’t always tell whether I’m enjoying it or just going through the motions because it’s what I know how to do.\n\nSome things still feel good in little flashes, like a piece of music catching me off guard or a moment in rehearsal where my body remembers something before my mind gets in the way. Like I can hear a song and know it’s beautiful, or I can be in a studio and know that I used to feel lit up by that particular kind of movement or rhythm. More distant.\n\nSometimes there are moments where something breaks through a little, like if the music is just right or I’m not overthinking and my body kind of takes over. I don’t think I can say that cleanly, like, “this one thing gives me zero feeling.” It’s more that a lot of things feel dulled or kind of far away from me.\n\nThere are definitely activities where I’ll start them and realize I’m waiting for the feeling to come, and it just doesn’t really arrive the way it used to. Like I can be doing something familiar and I know, intellectually, that I used to love it, but in the moment it feels more like completing a task than receiving anything from it. But I don’t know if that’s the same as absolutely no pleasure.\n\nI guess if you mean things that feel completely empty every single time, I haven’t noticed one clear category like that. It varies. Some days something gives me a tiny spark, and another day the same thing feels like nothing. So… not absolutely no pleasure, maybe. More like inconsistent, muted, hard to access.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. I don’t feel like I’ve completely lost interest, because if I had, I don’t think I’d still be trying to do any of it. Dance, music, being in creative spaces—those things still matter to me, and I still sort of reach toward them automatically because they’ve been part of me for so long.\n\nBut the interest doesn’t feel as alive as it used to. It’s more like I remember caring, and I’m trying to stay connected to that version of myself. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do this,” or “This used to make me feel like myself,” and then when I actually do it, the feeling is faint or delayed or just not really there in the way I expect.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped caring altogether. It’s not like I look at everything I used to love and feel nothing across the board. But I do feel less pulled toward things. Less excited. It feels like the interest is still there somewhere, but it’s harder to access.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I try not to say it too dramatically, because I know people keep telling me I’m being hard on myself, but yes, I do.\n\nI keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, almost like if I replay every decision enough times I’ll find the exact moment where I ruined it. And then I start thinking about the dancers, and how much they put into it, and the funders who trusted me, and the people in the community who were waiting for it or had some connection to it. It just feels like I let all of them down.\n\nAnd the confusing part is that people have actually been kinder about it than I feel like I deserve. They’ll say, “These things happen,” or “We understand,” or they’ll try to reassure me, and I can hear the words, but I don’t really believe them all the way. I keep thinking they’re just being gracious, or they don’t want to make me feel worse, or they don’t fully understand how much I failed them.\n\nSo yes, I blame myself a lot. Maybe more than is useful. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No. Not a specific plan.

I mean, I’ve had those vague “I don’t want to be here in this feeling” thoughts, like I said, but I haven’t gone into planning anything out. I haven’t sat down and thought, “This is what I would do,” or picked a method, or made arrangements, or anything like that.

It’s more like… when I’m overwhelmed, my mind goes toward wanting a pause button, or wanting to not have to be conscious of it for a while. But I don’t think that’s the same as having an actual plan to hurt myself. And I haven’t had that.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F33.0, F33.1, F33.2, F33.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… what brings me in is that I’ve been waking up with this really heavy, hollow kind of sadness. It’s hard to describe without sounding dramatic, but it feels like the studio lights are on and everything is technically there, but there’s no music in me anymore. Like I get up and I’m supposed to be myself, supposed to move through the day, and there’s just this emptiness sitting in my chest. It’s been weighing on me enough that I thought I should talk to someone instead of just trying to push through it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, hollow kind of sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. Not just a bad week or a rough patch that passed. It kind of settled in and stayed. I keep trying to mark when it started exactly, like whether there was a clear before-and-after moment, but it’s more like I look back and realize I’ve been carrying this heaviness for most of the past year. So, yes—around nine months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're still able to enjoy activities or things that you used to like before this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I still do some of the things I used to like, but I don’t know if I’m enjoying them in the same way, if that makes sense. Dance has always been such a big part of how I understand myself, so even when I’m feeling heavy, I can still recognize that movement matters to me. But lately I can’t always tell whether I’m enjoying it or just going through the motions because it’s what I know how to do.\n\nSome things still feel good in little flashes, like a piece of music catching me off guard or a moment in rehearsal where my body remembers something before my mind gets in the way. But it’s not consistent. I wouldn’t say everything is completely flat, but it’s… muted, I guess. Like the volume is turned down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, the way you used to before this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there’s anything that feels exactly the way it used to. That’s the part that’s hard for me to pin down, because I can still appreciate things, I think. Like I can hear a song and know it’s beautiful, or I can be in a studio and know that I used to feel lit up by that particular kind of movement or rhythm. But the actual pleasure of it is… I don’t know, thinner? More distant.\n\nSometimes there are moments where something breaks through a little, like if the music is just right or I’m not overthinking and my body kind of takes over. But it doesn’t last, and it doesn’t feel as full as it used to. So I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure there are activities I enjoy in the old way right now. There are just pieces of enjoyment, little flashes, but not the same steady feeling I remember.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel absolutely no pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: Absolutely no pleasure… I’m not sure. I don’t think I can say that cleanly, like, “this one thing gives me zero feeling.” It’s more that a lot of things feel dulled or kind of far away from me.\n\nThere are definitely activities where I’ll start them and realize I’m waiting for the feeling to come, and it just doesn’t really arrive the way it used to. Like I can be doing something familiar and I know, intellectually, that I used to love it, but in the moment it feels more like completing a task than receiving anything from it. But I don’t know if that’s the same as absolutely no pleasure.\n\nI guess if you mean things that feel completely empty every single time, I haven’t noticed one clear category like that. It varies. Some days something gives me a tiny spark, and another day the same thing feels like nothing. So… not absolutely no pleasure, maybe. More like inconsistent, muted, hard to access.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. I don’t feel like I’ve completely lost interest, because if I had, I don’t think I’d still be trying to do any of it. Dance, music, being in creative spaces—those things still matter to me, and I still sort of reach toward them automatically because they’ve been part of me for so long.\n\nBut the interest doesn’t feel as alive as it used to. It’s more like I remember caring, and I’m trying to stay connected to that version of myself. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do this,” or “This used to make me feel like myself,” and then when I actually do it, the feeling is faint or delayed or just not really there in the way I expect.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped caring altogether. It’s not like I look at everything I used to love and feel nothing across the board. But I do feel less pulled toward things. Less excited. More like I have to remind myself to engage instead of naturally wanting to. It feels like the interest is still there somewhere, but it’s harder to access.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I try not to say it too dramatically, because I know people keep telling me I’m being hard on myself, but yes, I do.\n\nI keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, almost like if I replay every decision enough times I’ll find the exact moment where I ruined it. And then I start thinking about the dancers, and how much they put into it, and the funders who trusted me, and the people in the community who were waiting for it or had some connection to it. It just feels like I let all of them down.\n\nAnd the confusing part is that people have actually been kinder about it than I feel like I deserve. They’ll say, “These things happen,” or “We understand,” or they’ll try to reassure me, and I can hear the words, but I don’t really believe them all the way. I keep thinking they’re just being gracious, or they don’t want to make me feel worse, or they don’t fully understand how much I failed them.\n\nSo yes, I blame myself a lot. Maybe more than is useful. It’s not just regret, it feels like this heavy sense that I should have held everything together better.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead during these past nine months?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly.\n\nI don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like I’ve been sitting there deciding how I would hurt myself. But there have been moments where I’ve felt so tired of being inside my own head that I’ve thought, not exactly “I want to die,” but more like… “I don’t want to keep feeling this.” Or “I wish I could disappear for a while.” It’s hard for me to know if that counts.\n\nAnd I feel nervous saying even that, because I don’t want it to sound more dramatic than it is. I haven’t been actively trying to hurt myself. It’s more passive, I guess—like wanting everything to stop, or wanting to not have to wake up and deal with the same weight again. But then another part of me is still aware that I don’t actually want to leave people or do something irreversible.\n\nSo… yes, maybe in a vague way at times, but not with a plan. More like thoughts of escape than thoughts of actually harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or the way you move throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. My energy feels… unreliable, I guess. Like I can wake up and think, okay, I’m going to do one or two very basic things today, and then one small thing takes so much more out of me than it should.\n\nEven answering one email to a dancer can leave me feeling wiped out, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud because it’s just an email. But I’ll sit there staring at it, trying to choose the right words, feeling the weight of it, and by the time I’ve sent it I feel like I’ve already used up most of what I had for the day.\n\nAnd showering has become this whole thing too, especially with the ankle brace. It’s not just “take a shower.” It’s managing the brace, standing carefully, trying not to slip, drying off, getting everything back on, and afterward I sometimes feel like I need to lie down. But the frustrating part is that lying down doesn’t really recharge me the way I expect it to. I’ll rest, but I don’t feel restored. It’s more like I’m still drained, just horizontal.\n\nSo yes, my energy is lower. I don’t know if my movement is slower exactly, but I feel like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself consistently, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my body feels different, and my rehearsal clothes are looser than they used to be. Like leggings and tops that normally sit snugly are sort of hanging on me more, especially around my waist and hips.\n\nPart of it was that earlier on, when I was really buzzing with ideas and trying to keep all these creative threads going, I’d just forget to eat. Not on purpose exactly — I’d look up and realize I’d skipped lunch, or I’d had coffee and maybe a few bites of something and somehow convinced myself that counted.\n\nAnd now it’s almost the opposite, but the result is similar. I’ll know I should eat, but food just doesn’t have much appeal. It tastes kind of like cardboard, even things I usually like. I’ll make something and then just push it around or eat enough to say I ate. So yes, I think I’ve lost some weight over these months, even if I don’t know the exact amount.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a specific plan to harm yourself during these past nine months?\n\nPatient: No. Not a specific plan.\n\nI mean, I’ve had those vague “I don’t want to be here in this feeling” thoughts, like I said, but I haven’t gone into planning anything out. I haven’t sat down and thought, “This is what I would do,” or picked a method, or made arrangements, or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… when I’m overwhelmed, my mind goes toward wanting a pause button, or wanting to not have to be conscious of it for a while. But I don’t think that’s the same as having an actual plan to hurt myself. And I haven’t had that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

the wired stretch when I was barely sleeping and racing through plans. Since my mood dropped, I still feel guilty and slowed down, but I haven’t heard the voice or believed the signs were directing me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I fell behind on teaching prep and missed a grant deadline, and my studio got so chaotic that collaborators started handling basic scheduling for me. I’m still showing up for essentials, but I’m not functioning like I normally do as a choreographer or teacher.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 months\n  manifestation: This whole pattern started after my ankle injury and has been going on for about two months now.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 9 months\n  manifestation: The depressed part has been present for about nine months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 34 / dancer_or_choreographer / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S024\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or the way you move throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. My energy feels… unreliable, I guess. Like I can wake up and think, okay, I’m going to do one or two very basic things today, and then one small thing takes so much more out of me than it should.\n\nEven answering one email to a dancer can leave me feeling wiped out, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud because it’s just an email. But I’ll sit there staring at it, trying to choose the right words, feeling the weight of it, and by the time I’ve sent it I feel like I’ve already used up most of what I had for the day.\n\nAnd showering has become this whole thing too, especially with the ankle brace. But the frustrating part is that lying down doesn’t really recharge me the way I expect it to. I’ll rest, but I don’t feel restored. It’s more like I’m still drained, just horizontal.\n\nSo yes, my energy is lower. I haven’t been weighing myself consistently, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my body feels different, and my rehearsal clothes are looser than they used to be. Like leggings and tops that normally sit snugly are sort of hanging on me more, especially around my waist and hips.\n\nPart of it was that earlier on, when I was really buzzing with ideas and trying to keep all these creative threads going, I’d just forget to eat. I’ll know I should eat, but food just doesn’t have much appeal. It tastes kind of like cardboard, even things I usually like. I’ll make something and then just push it around or eat enough to say I ate. I haven’t sat down and thought, “This is what I would do,” or picked a method, or made arrangements, or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… when I’m overwhelmed, my mind goes toward wanting a pause button, or wanting to not have to be conscious of it for a while. But I don’t think that’s the same as having an actual plan to hurt myself. And I haven’t had that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced any thoughts or beliefs that seem unreal or detached from reality during these past nine months?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. I… yes, I think that did happen, although it’s embarrassing to say it plainly.

There was a period where I was completely sure the mountains had sort of… chosen me, or singled me out, to make this historic piece. Not just inspired me in the normal artistic way, like “oh, the landscape is beautiful, I want to respond to it.” It felt much more direct than that. Like they were communicating with me, or arranging things around me, and I was the only one who understood what was being asked.

I started taking things as instructions. Trail signs, for example — not just as signs, but as if the wording or the arrows were meant specifically for me and for the piece. Weather reports too. If there was wind coming in or a storm warning, I’d think it was telling me something about timing or movement or what I was supposed to make next. Even strangers’ comments, like someone passing by saying something totally ordinary, I would hear it as… not ordinary. Like it had been placed there for me.

At the time it didn’t feel unreal. That’s the hard part. It felt incredibly meaningful and urgent, like I’d been entrusted with something important. Looking back now, I can see how detached from reality it sounds. But while it was happening, I was really convinced.

Like I get up and I’m supposed to be myself, supposed to move through the day, and there’s just this emptiness sitting in my chest. It’s been weighing on me enough that I thought I should talk to someone instead of just trying to push through it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, hollow kind of sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. Not just a bad week or a rough patch that passed. It kind of settled in and stayed. I keep trying to mark when it started exactly, like whether there was a clear before-and-after moment, but it’s more like I look back and realize I’ve been carrying this heaviness for most of the past year. So, yes—around nine months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're still able to enjoy activities or things that you used to like before this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I’m… not completely sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean, I still do some of the things I used to like, but I don’t know if I’m enjoying them in the same way, if that makes sense. Dance has always been such a big part of how I understand myself, so even when I’m feeling heavy, I can still recognize that movement matters to me. But lately I can’t always tell whether I’m enjoying it or just going through the motions because it’s what I know how to do.\n\nSome things still feel good in little flashes, like a piece of music catching me off guard or a moment in rehearsal where my body remembers something before my mind gets in the way. But it’s not consistent. I wouldn’t say everything is completely flat, but it’s… muted, I guess. Like the volume is turned down.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still find pleasure in, the way you used to before this sadness started?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that there’s anything that feels exactly the way it used to. That’s the part that’s hard for me to pin down, because I can still appreciate things, I think. Like I can hear a song and know it’s beautiful, or I can be in a studio and know that I used to feel lit up by that particular kind of movement or rhythm. But the actual pleasure of it is… I don’t know, thinner? More distant.\n\nSometimes there are moments where something breaks through a little, like if the music is just right or I’m not overthinking and my body kind of takes over. But it doesn’t last, and it doesn’t feel as full as it used to. So I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure there are activities I enjoy in the old way right now. There are just pieces of enjoyment, little flashes, but not the same steady feeling I remember.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities where you feel absolutely no pleasure at all?\n\nPatient: Absolutely no pleasure… I’m not sure. I don’t think I can say that cleanly, like, “this one thing gives me zero feeling.” It’s more that a lot of things feel dulled or kind of far away from me.\n\nThere are definitely activities where I’ll start them and realize I’m waiting for the feeling to come, and it just doesn’t really arrive the way it used to. Like I can be doing something familiar and I know, intellectually, that I used to love it, but in the moment it feels more like completing a task than receiving anything from it. But I don’t know if that’s the same as absolutely no pleasure.\n\nI guess if you mean things that feel completely empty every single time, I haven’t noticed one clear category like that. It varies. Some days something gives me a tiny spark, and another day the same thing feels like nothing. So… not absolutely no pleasure, maybe. More like inconsistent, muted, hard to access.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. I don’t feel like I’ve completely lost interest, because if I had, I don’t think I’d still be trying to do any of it. Dance, music, being in creative spaces—those things still matter to me, and I still sort of reach toward them automatically because they’ve been part of me for so long.\n\nBut the interest doesn’t feel as alive as it used to. It’s more like I remember caring, and I’m trying to stay connected to that version of myself. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do this,” or “This used to make me feel like myself,” and then when I actually do it, the feeling is faint or delayed or just not really there in the way I expect.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped caring altogether. It’s not like I look at everything I used to love and feel nothing across the board. But I do feel less pulled toward things. Less excited. More like I have to remind myself to engage instead of naturally wanting to. It feels like the interest is still there somewhere, but it’s harder to access.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I try not to say it too dramatically, because I know people keep telling me I’m being hard on myself, but yes, I do.\n\nI keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, almost like if I replay every decision enough times I’ll find the exact moment where I ruined it. And then I start thinking about the dancers, and how much they put into it, and the funders who trusted me, and the people in the community who were waiting for it or had some connection to it. It just feels like I let all of them down.\n\nAnd the confusing part is that people have actually been kinder about it than I feel like I deserve. They’ll say, “These things happen,” or “We understand,” or they’ll try to reassure me, and I can hear the words, but I don’t really believe them all the way. I keep thinking they’re just being gracious, or they don’t want to make me feel worse, or they don’t fully understand how much I failed them.\n\nSo yes, I blame myself a lot. Maybe more than is useful. It’s not just regret, it feels like this heavy sense that I should have held everything together better.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead during these past nine months?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly.\n\nI don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like I’ve been sitting there deciding how I would hurt myself. But there have been moments where I’ve felt so tired of being inside my own head that I’ve thought, not exactly “I want to die,” but more like… “I don’t want to keep feeling this.” Or “I wish I could disappear for a while.” It’s hard for me to know if that counts.\n\nAnd I feel nervous saying even that, because I don’t want it to sound more dramatic than it is. I haven’t been actively trying to hurt myself. It’s more passive, I guess—like wanting everything to stop, or wanting to not have to wake up and deal with the same weight again. But then another part of me is still aware that I don’t actually want to leave people or do something irreversible.\n\nSo… yes, maybe in a vague way at times, but not with a plan. More like thoughts of escape than thoughts of actually harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or the way you move throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It’s not just “take a shower.” It’s managing the brace, standing carefully, trying not to slip, drying off, getting everything back on, and afterward I sometimes feel like I need to lie down. But the frustrating part is that lying down doesn’t really recharge me the way I expect it to. I’ll rest, but I don’t feel restored. It’s more like I’m still drained, just horizontal.\n\nSo yes, my energy is lower. Dance, music, being in creative spaces—those things still matter to me, and I still sort of reach toward them automatically because they’ve been part of me for so long.\n\nBut the interest doesn’t feel as alive as it used to. It’s more like I remember caring, and I’m trying to stay connected to that version of myself. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should want to do this,” or “This used to make me feel like myself,” and then when I actually do it, the feeling is faint or delayed or just not really there in the way I expect.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve stopped caring altogether. It’s not like I look at everything I used to love and feel nothing across the board. But I do feel less pulled toward things. Less excited. More like I have to remind myself to engage instead of naturally wanting to. It feels like the interest is still there somewhere, but it’s harder to access.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean… I try not to say it too dramatically, because I know people keep telling me I’m being hard on myself, but yes, I do.\n\nI keep going back over the cancelled performances in my head, almost like if I replay every decision enough times I’ll find the exact moment where I ruined it. And then I start thinking about the dancers, and how much they put into it, and the funders who trusted me, and the people in the community who were waiting for it or had some connection to it. It just feels like I let all of them down.\n\nAnd the confusing part is that people have actually been kinder about it than I feel like I deserve. They’ll say, “These things happen,” or “We understand,” or they’ll try to reassure me, and I can hear the words, but I don’t really believe them all the way. I keep thinking they’re just being gracious, or they don’t want to make me feel worse, or they don’t fully understand how much I failed them.\n\nSo yes, I blame myself a lot. Maybe more than is useful. It’s not just regret, it feels like this heavy sense that I should have held everything together better.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about harming yourself or wished you were dead during these past nine months?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that neatly.\n\nI don’t think I’ve had a clear plan or anything like that, if that’s what you mean. It’s not like I’ve been sitting there deciding how I would hurt myself. But there have been moments where I’ve felt so tired of being inside my own head that I’ve thought, not exactly “I want to die,” but more like… “I don’t want to keep feeling this.” Or “I wish I could disappear for a while.” It’s hard for me to know if that counts.\n\nAnd I feel nervous saying even that, because I don’t want it to sound more dramatic than it is. I haven’t been actively trying to hurt myself. It’s more passive, I guess—like wanting everything to stop, or wanting to not have to wake up and deal with the same weight again. But then another part of me is still aware that I don’t actually want to leave people or do something irreversible.\n\nSo… yes, maybe in a vague way at times, but not with a plan. More like thoughts of escape than thoughts of actually harming myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy level or the way you move throughout the day?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. My energy feels… unreliable, I guess. Like I can wake up and think, okay, I’m going to do one or two very basic things today, and then one small thing takes so much more out of me than it should.\n\nEven answering one email to a dancer can leave me feeling wiped out, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud because it’s just an email. But I’ll sit there staring at it, trying to choose the right words, feeling the weight of it, and by the time I’ve sent it I feel like I’ve already used up most of what I had for the day.\n\nAnd showering has become this whole thing too, especially with the ankle brace. It’s not just “take a shower.” It’s managing the brace, standing carefully, trying not to slip, drying off, getting everything back on, and afterward I sometimes feel like I need to lie down. But the frustrating part is that lying down doesn’t really recharge me the way I expect it to. I’ll rest, but I don’t feel restored. It’s more like I’m still drained, just horizontal.\n\nSo yes, my energy is lower. I don’t know if my movement is slower exactly, but I feel like everything takes more effort than it should.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself consistently, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my body feels different, and my rehearsal clothes are looser than they used to be. Like leggings and tops that normally sit snugly are sort of hanging on me more, especially around my waist and hips.\n\nPart of it was that earlier on, when I was really buzzing with ideas and trying to keep all these creative threads going, I’d just forget to eat. Not on purpose exactly — I’d look up and realize I’d skipped lunch, or I’d had coffee and maybe a few bites of something and somehow convinced myself that counted.\n\nAnd now it’s almost the opposite, but the result is similar. I’ll know I should eat, but food just doesn’t have much appeal. It tastes kind of like cardboard, even things I usually like. I’ll make something and then just push it around or eat enough to say I ate. So yes, I think I’ve lost some weight over these months, even if I don’t know the exact amount.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a specific plan to harm yourself during these past nine months?\n\nPatient: No. Not a specific plan.\n\nI mean, I’ve had those vague “I don’t want to be here in this feeling” thoughts, like I said, but I haven’t gone into planning anything out. I haven’t sat down and thought, “This is what I would do,” or picked a method, or made arrangements, or anything like that.\n\nIt’s more like… when I’m overwhelmed, my mind goes toward wanting a pause button, or wanting to not have to be conscious of it for a while. But I don’t think that’s the same as having an actual plan to hurt myself. And I haven’t had that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any thoughts or beliefs that seem unreal or detached from reality during these past nine months?\n\nPatient: Yes. I… yes, I think that did happen, although it’s embarrassing to say it plainly.\n\nThere was a period where I was completely sure the mountains had sort of… chosen me, or singled me out, to make this historic piece. Not just inspired me in the normal artistic way, like “oh, the landscape is beautiful, I want to respond to it.” It felt much more direct than that. Like they were communicating with me, or arranging things around me, and I was the only one who understood what was being asked.\n\nI started taking things as instructions. Trail signs, for example — not just as signs, but as if the wording or the arrows were meant specifically for me and for the piece. Weather reports too. If there was wind coming in or a storm warning, I’d think it was telling me something about timing or movement or what I was supposed to make next. Even strangers’ comments, like someone passing by saying something totally ordinary, I would hear it as… not ordinary. Like it had been placed there for me.\n\nAt the time it didn’t feel unreal. That’s the hard part. It felt incredibly meaningful and urgent, like I’d been entrusted with something important. Looking back now, I can see how detached from reality it sounds. But while it was happening, I was really convinced.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Aside from that… I don’t think there was a separate belief, exactly. It was mostly all part of that same thing with the mountains and the piece.

Like, it spread into a lot of ordinary details, if that makes sense. It wasn’t only me standing there looking at the mountains and thinking they were meaningful. It was more like the whole environment started feeling organized around that message. Trail signs felt like they were directing me personally, not just hikers. Weather reports felt like instructions about timing or what the movement should be. And even little comments from strangers would land as if they were meant for me specifically, like they’d been placed in my path.

So… no, I don’t know that I had another totally different unreal belief. It was that same conviction, but it kind of attached itself to everything around me for a while.

It’s hard to describe without sounding dramatic, but it feels like the studio lights are on and everything is technically there, but there’s no music in me anymore. Like I get up and I’m supposed to be myself, supposed to move through the day, and there’s just this emptiness sitting in my chest. It’s been weighing on me enough that I thought I should talk to someone instead of just trying to push through it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this heavy, hollow kind of sadness?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months, I think. Not just a bad week or a rough patch that passed. It kind of settled in and stayed.
